[anchor: introduction]

# 3.1. Introduction

The core assembly thermal hydraulics treatment in SAS4A/SASSYS-1
includes the calculation of fuel, cladding, coolant, and structure
temperatures, as well as coolant flow rates and pressure distributions.
This treatment includes melting of the fuel and cladding. Boiling of the
coolant is also handled, as described in Chapter 12. The relocation of
fuel and cladding after pin disruption is described in Chapter 13, Chapter 14,
and Chapter 16; and relocation of molten fuel before pin disruption is described
in Chapter 15.

Prior to version 3.0, all of the core subassembly models in
SAS4A/SASSYS-1 were single pin models: a single fuel pin and its
associated coolant were used to represent a subassembly; and pin-to-pin
variations within a subassembly were ignored. A multiple pin option has
been added to the code in version 3.0. A number of pins and their
associated coolant can now be used to represent a subassembly, so
variations within a subassembly can be accounted for. Currently the
multiple pin option is only available for single-phase thermal
hydraulics; it does not handle coolant boiling, in-pin fuel relocation,
or pin disruption. Therefore, typical SAS4A/SASSYS-1 cases that do not
get into coolant boiling can be handled with the multiple-pin model, but
typical core disruption cases can only be handled with single pin
models.

Although SAS4A/SASSYS-1 is mainly a transient code, both steady-state
and transient temperatures and coolant pressures are calculated. The
steady-state solutions are obtained from the transient equations after
dropping all time derivatives. In general, the steady-state solutions in
the single pin per subassembly model are not obtained by running a
transient calculation at constant power and flow until the results
approach a steady-state solution. Instead, the steady-state temperatures
are obtained rapidly from a direct solution based on conservation of
energy and the use of the same spatial finite differencing as used in
the transient. On the other hand, a direct steady-state solution for the
multiple pin option would be much more complicated, especially if
subassembly-to-subassembly heat transfer is included. Therefore, a null
transient with powers and flows held constant is used to obtain
steady-state conditions for the multiple pin option.

The thermal hydraulics calculations are carried out in a number of
separate modules, and each module is designed for a specific type of
calculation. A steady-state thermal hydraulics module provides the
initial conditions for the transient. The transient temperatures are
calculated in a pre-voiding module (TSHTRN) until the onset of boiling.
After the onset of boiling, the fuel-pin temperatures are calculated in
a separate module (TSHTRV) that couples with the boiling module.

The core thermal-hydraulic routines interact with a number of other
modules, as shown in Figure 3.1.1. Before the onset of voiding, TSHTRN
calculates the coolant temperatures used in the hydraulic calculations,
whereas the hydraulics routines calculate the coolant flow rates used in
TSHTRN. After the onset of voiding, coolant temperatures are calculated
in TSBOIL, and this module supplies the heat flux at the cladding outer
surface or the fuel outer surface to TSHTRV. TSBOIL uses the cladding
temperatures from TSHTRV in its coolant temperature calculations. The
point kinetics module supplies the power level used in the heat-transfer
routines, and the heat-transfer routines supply the Doppler feedback
reactivity as well as other temperature-dependent reactivity feedback.
TSBOIL supplies the voiding reactivity. The inlet plenum temperature
computed by PRIMAR-4 is used in calculating the inlet temperature for
TSHTRN or TSBOIL, and TSHTRN or TSBOIL provides the subassembly outlet
temperatures used by PRIMAR-4 to compute the outlet plenum temperature.
If flow reversal occurs in a subassembly, then the outlet plenum
temperature computed by PRIMAR-4 is used in calculating the coolant
temperature at the top of the subassembly, and the temperature computed
by TSHTRN or TSBOIL for the coolant leaving the bottom of the
subassembly is used by PRIMAR-4 to calculate the inlet plenum
temperature. PRIMAR-4 supplies the inlet and outlet plenum pressures
that drive the coolant hydraulics calculations, and the core channel
flows are provided to PRIMAR-4 by TSBOIL and the pre-voiding hydraulics.
The initial coolant flow rate and pressure distribution are supplied to
TSBOIL by the pre-voiding hydraulics routines.

The transient calculations in the code used a multi-level time step
approach, with separate time steps for each module. For the
heat-transfer routines, all temperatures are known at the beginning of a
heat-transfer step, and the routines calculate the new temperatures at
the end of the step. The heat-transfer time step can be longer than the
coolant time step or the PRIMAR time step, but the heat-transfer time
step can be no longer than the main time step that is used for
reactivity feedback and main printouts.

Figure 3.1.2 shows the flow through the pre-voiding core channel thermal
hydraulics driver, TSCL0. This routine is entered once for each channel
during each coolant time step. The coolant flow rates are calculated
before the heat-transfer module (TSHTRN) is called. TSHTRN is only
called if the current coolant time step completes a heat-transfer time
step. The voiding model, TSHTRV, is described in Chapter 12.

In this chapter, Section 3.2 describes the mesh structure used for
heat-transfer calculations. Then, Section 3.3 describes the pre-boiling
transient heat-transfer calculations, followed by the steady-state
thermal hydraulics calculations in Section 3.4. The pre-voiding
transient heat transfer is discussed before the steady-state thermal
hydraulics for two reasons. First, the code is primarily a transient
code, so the transient calculations are more important. Second, the
finite difference approximations were made with the transient
calculations in mind, and the steady-state solution was formulated to be
consistent with the approximations used in the transient. Section 3.5
describes TSHTRV, the fuel-pin heat-transfer calculations in the boiling
module. Section 3.6 describes the treatment of the bond-gap conductance
between the fuel and the cladding. Section 3.7 describes modifications
to the fuel pin heat transfer calculations for PLUTO2 and LEVITATE. The
heat transfer time step control is described in Section 3.8. Section 3.9
describes steady-state and pre-voiding transient hydraulics. Section 3.10 describes the multiple pin option. Subassembly-to-subassembly heat
transfers described in Section 3.11. Section 3.12 describes interaction
with other modules. It is followed by sections providing subroutine
descriptions and flowcharts, subchannel model treatment details, thermal
properties of fuel and cladding, and a description of the input to, and
output from, the thermal hydraulic routines.

Figure 3.1.1 Interactions of Thermal-Hydraulic Routines with Other Modules

Figure 3.1.2 Flowchart for the Pre-Voiding Core Channel Thermal Hydraulics Driver (Subroutine TSCL0)

[anchor: sas-channel-approach]

# 3.2. SAS4A/SASSYS-1 Channel Approach

SAS4A/SASSYS-1 is capable of using a multi-channel treatment. Each
channel represents a fuel pin, its associated coolant, and a fraction of
the subassembly duct wall, as indicated in Figure 3.2.1. Usually, a
channel is used to represent an average pin in a fuel subassembly or a
group of similar subassemblies. A channel can also be used to represent
a blanket assembly or a control-rod channel, and the hottest pin in a
subassembly can be represented instead of the average pin. Different
channels can be used to account for radial and azimuthal power
variations within the core, as well as variations in coolant flow
orificing and fuel burn-up. In the multiple pin option, more than one
channel can be used to represent a subassembly.

[anchor: axial-mesh-structure]

## 3.2.1. Axial Mesh Structure

A channel usually represents the whole length of the subassembly, from
coolant inlet to coolant outlet. A number of axial zones are used, as
indicated in Figure 3.2.2. One zone represents the fuel-pin section,
including the core, axial blankets, and gas plenum. Other zones
represent reflector regions above and below the pin section. A maximum
of 7 zones can be used in a channel. In general, radial dimensions and
thermal properties are constant with a reflector zone. The pin section
zone is treated separately in considerably more detail than the
reflector zones. The gas plenum can be either above or below the core.

Figure 3.2.3 shows the axial mesh structure used for a channel. The
coolant and structure nodes run the whole length of the channel. The
coolant nodes are staggered with respect to the fuel, cladding,
reflector, and structure nodes. Using coolant temperatures defined at
the axial boundaries between cladding and structure nodes makes it
easier to calculate accurate coolant temperatures. If non-uniform axial
mesh sizes are used, a simple finite differencing of the coolant
temperature equation gives accurate coolant temperatures with a
staggered mesh, whereas if the coolant nodes were at the middle of the
cladding nodes, then obtaining accurate coolant temperatures would
require extra terms in the finite differencing of the coolant
temperature equation.

Figure 3.2.1 SAS4A/SASSYS-1 Channel Treatment

Figure 3.2.2 Axial Zones in a SAS4A/SASSYS-1 Channel

Figure 3.2.3 Schematic of SAS4A/SASSYS-1 Channel Discretization

[anchor: radial-mesh-structure]

## 3.2.2. Radial Mesh Structure

[anchor: core-and-blanket-region]

### 3.2.2.1. Core and Blanket Region

Figure 3.2.4 shows the radial mesh structure used for temperature
calculations in the core and blanket regions. This figure represents one
axial node. Between four and eleven radial nodes are used in the fuel,
three in the cladding, one in the coolant, and two in the structure. In
the fuel, the nodes can be set up on either an equal radial difference
basis or an equal mass basis. In either case, the first and last nodes
are half-size. For a given number of nodes, an equal radial difference
mesh will usually give more accurate center-line temperatures, but equal
mass nodes are sometimes used to get more nodes in the outer part of the
fuel, where temperature gradients are steeper. Steady-state fuel
restructuring can change the node sizes. Also, during the transient
calculation, the radii will move with the fuel as it expands or
contracts due to temperature changes. After the steady-state
initialization, the mass of fuel associated with a radial node is
constant, at least until fuel-pin disruption and coupling is made to
PLUTO2, PINACLE, or LEVITATE.

The inner fuel node is at $r\ = \ 0$ if there is no central void.
Otherwise, it is at the fuel inner surface. The outer fuel node is at
the fuel outer surface.

The “structure” represents each pin’s share of the duct wall. A wrapper
wire can be lumped in with either the cladding or the structure.

[anchor: gas-plenum-region]

### 3.2.2.2. Gas Plenum Region

The radial mesh structure used in the gas plenum region is shown in
Figure 3.2.5. The plenum gas is represented by a single axial and radial
node. This gas is in contact with a number of axial cladding nodes. At
each axial node, there is one radial node in the cladding, one in the
coolant, and two in the structure.

[anchor: reflector-regions]

### 3.2.2.3. Reflector Regions

The radial mesh structure in an axial node in a reflector region is
shown in Figure 3.2.6. Two nodes are used in the reflector, one in the
coolant, and two in the structure.

Figure 3.2.4 Radial Temperature Nodes, Core and Axial Blanket Regions

Figure 3.2.5 Radial Temperature Nodes, Gas Plenum Region

Figure 3.2.6 Radial Temperature Nodes, Reflector Region

[anchor: pre-boiling-transient-heat-transfer-single-pin-model]

# 3.3. Pre-boiling Transient Heat Transfer, Single Pin Model

The transient fuel-pin temperature calculations in SAS4A/SASSYS-1 are
similar to the Crank-Nicolson scheme [3-1] used in SAS2A [3-2] and
SAS3D [3-3], but there are a number of significant differences. One
reason for these differences is to allow the use of larger heat-transfer
time steps with less computing time per step. Another reason is to
obtain greater accuracy and more precise energy balance.

In order to use very long heat-transfer time steps on one second or more
in the pre-voiding phase of a very slow transient, the fuel, cladding,
coolant, and structure temperatures at an axial node are computed
simultaneously in non-voiding cases. Therefore, coolant temperatures are
computed in the fuel-pin heat-transfer routine in the non-voiding
situation.

The coupling between TSHTRN and the pre-voiding coolant dynamics
routines is different from the coupling between TSHTRV and the voiding
routines, although in both cases the coolant dynamics or voiding
calculations are done before the fuel-pin heat-transfer calculations. In
the non-voiding case, extrapolated coolant temperatures are used to
obtain the temperature-dependent sodium properties used in the
calculation of the coolant flow rates and heat-transfer coefficients.
Then, TSHTRN uses these values to compute the coolant temperatures, and
these new coolant temperatures are used in the next extrapolation. In
the voiding routines, extrapolated cladding temperatures are used in the
implicit calculation of the heat flux from cladding to coolant to obtain
new coolant temperatures. The voiding routines then sum the integrated
heat flux from cladding to coolant at each axial node, and this
integrated heat flux at the cladding surface is used as a boundary
condition in TSHTRV.

TSHTRN and TSHTRV are somewhat simpler than the corresponding TSHTR in
SAS3A and SAS3D. In SAS3D, TSHTR contains extraneous material related to
other modules, such as initiating cladding and fuel motion; this
material is not included in the SAS4A/SASSYS-1 heat-transfer routines.
Also, in SAS3D, the fuel mass for each radial node of each axial node is
recomputed every time step from the temperature-dependent fuel density
and the node radii. In SAS4A/SASSYS-1 the node mass is computed in the
steady-state module and then stored for used in the transient. This node
mass is held constant until fuel relocation starts.

Another difference between the SAS3D and SASSYS-1 heat-transfer routines
is in the amount of vectorization of the algorithms and the coding. Some
of the calculations in the SAS3D routines happen to vectorize, but a
special effort was made to vectorize many more of the calculations in
the SAS4A/SASSYS-1 heat-transfer routines. In general, vectorizing
involves setting up arrays so that all elements in the array are
processed in the same way and so that the results of a given calculation
for one element in the array do not depend on the results for any other
element in the array. Vectorization is partly just a coding matter, but
it also involves choosing vectorizable algorithms or adapting algorithms
for vectorization.

To some extent, the descriptions in the following sections reflect the
emphasis on vectorization. Intermediate quantities used in the solution
are usually defined as elements in arrays, and, where possible, the
array elements are defined so that all of the elements in an array can
be treated in the same manner in the calculations. In order to emphasize
the array nature of the solution, when a new array is introduced, all of
the elements in the array are defined in the same section, even though
many of the elements are often not used until later sections.

As indicated above in Section 3.2.2, three different radial mesh
structures are used in the heat-transfer calculations: one for the core
and blanket region, one for the gas plenum region, and one for the
reflector regions. Different heat-transfer calculations are done for
each of these three regions.

[anchor: core-and-axial-blankets]

## 3.3.1. Core and Axial Blankets

[anchor: basic-equations]

### 3.3.1.1. Basic Equations

The basic transient heat-transfer equation within the fuel and within
the cladding is

$$\rho c \frac{\partial \text{T}}{\partial \text{t}} = \frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial \text{r}}\left( \text{kr} \frac{\partial \text{T}}{\partial \text{r}} \right) + Q$$ [eq 3.3-1]

where

 $T$ = temperature

 $\rho$ = density

 $c$ = specific heat

 $r$ = radius

 $k$ = thermal conductivity

 $t$ = time

and

 $Q$ = heat source per unit volume

Melting of fuel and cladding is treated using a melting range, bounded
by solidus and liquidus temperatures, $T$ sol and
 $T$ liq, respectively, and a heat of fusion,
 $U$ melt. Separate values of $T$ sol,
 $T$ liq, and $U$ melt are used for the fuel
and cladding. In the melting range, Eq. (3.3-1) is modified and becomes

$$\rho c_{\text{m}}\frac{\partial \text{T}}{\partial \text{t}} = \frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial \text{r}}\left( \text{kr}\frac{\partial \text{T}}{\partial \text{r}} \right) + Q$$ [eq 3.3-2]

where

$$c_{\text{m}} = \frac{U_{\text{melt}}}{T_{\text{liq}} - T_{\text{sol}}}$$ [eq 3.3-3]

In the actual solution of Eq. (3.3-2) for a time step, $c$ is used
instead of $c_{\text{m}}$ in the calculation of the temperature change
for the step. Then, if the temperatures are in the melting range, the
computed temperature change is modified to account for the heat of
fusion, as described in Section 3.3.5.

The heat flux, $q_{\text{fe}}$ , from the fuel outer surface to the
cladding inner surface contains both a bond gap conductance term,
 $h_{\text{b}}$ , and a radiation term:

$$q_{\text{fe}} = h_{\text{b}}\left\lbrack T\left( \mathrm{\text{NT}} \right) - T\left( \text{NE}'' \right) \right\rbrack + \epsilon \sigma \left\lbrack T\left( \mathrm{\text{NT}} \right)^{4} - T\left( \text{NE}'' \right)^{4} \right\rbrack$$ [eq 3.3-4]

where

 $\epsilon$ = thermal emissivity of the fuel

 $\sigma$ = Stefan-Boltzmann constant

 $T$ (NT) = fuel outer surface temperature

and

 $T$ (NE’’) = cladding inner surface temperature

For the coolant, the basic heat-transfer equation is

$$\rho c ~A_{\text{c}}\frac{\partial \text{T}}{\partial \text{t}} + \frac{\partial}{\partial \text{z}}\left( \text{wcT} \right) = \left( Q_{\text{c}} + Q_{\text{ec}} + Q_{\text{sc}} \right)A_{\text{c}}$$ [eq 3.3-5]

where $A_{\text{c}}$ = coolant flow area. The heat, $Q_{\text{c}}$ ,
produced directly in the coolant is computed from the fraction,
 $\gamma_{\text{c}}$ , of the total energy production that goes into
neutron and gamma heating of the coolant:

$$Q_{\text{c}} = \frac{\gamma_{\text{c}}\overline{P}(j)}{A_{\text{c}}\Delta z(j)}$$ [eq 3.3-6]

where

 $\overline{P}\left( j \right)$ = total heat production
rate in axial node j

and

 $\Delta z(j)$ = axial node height

The heat flow from the cladding to the coolant, $Q_{\text{ec}}$ is
calculated as

$$Q_{\text{ec}} = h_{\text{c}}\left\lbrack T\left( \mathrm{N}\text{E}' \right) - T(\mathrm{\text{NC}}) \right\rbrack\frac{2 \pi r(\mathrm{N}\text{E}')\ }{A_{\text{c}}}$$ [eq 3.3-7]

and the heat flow from structure to coolant, $Q_{\text{sc}}$ , is
calculated as

$$Q_{\text{sc}} = h_{\text{c}}\left\lbrack T\left( \mathrm{\text{NSI}} \right) - T(\mathrm{\text{NC}}) \right\rbrack\frac{S_{\text{st}}}{A_{\text{c}}}$$ [eq 3.3-8]

where $S_{\text{st}}$ is the perimeter of the structure. The
coolant heat-transfer coefficient, $h_{c}$ , is calculated using

$$\frac{h_{\text{c}}D_{\text{h}}}{k} = c_{1}\left\lbrack \frac{D_{\text{h}}~\text{w c}}{\text{k}~A_{\text{c}}} \right\rbrack^{c_{2}} + ~c_{3}$$ [eq 3.3-9]

which is a form used in correlations for convective heat-transfer
coefficients for low Prandtl number fluids, such as liquid metal [3-4].
The user supplied constants $c_1$ , $c_2$ , and
 $c_3$ depend on the particular correlation used.

The structure is treated with a one-dimensional heat conduction
equation:

$$\rho c \frac{\partial \text{T}}{\partial \text{t}} = \frac{\partial}{\partial \text{x}}\left\lbrack k\ \frac{\partial \text{T}}{\partial \text{x}} \right\rbrack + Q$$ [eq 3.3-10]

The treatment of the heat source, $Q$ , in the structure is
discussed in Section 3.3.1.2.8.

[anchor: finite-difference-equations]

### 3.3.1.2. Finite Difference Equations

Finite differencing in both space and time is used for the transient
heat-transfer calculations. The radial mesh structure is described in
Section 3.2.2 above. In the equations below, the time t represents the
beginning of the temp step, and $\Delta t$ is the step size. The parameters
 $\theta_1$ and $\theta_2$ determine the degree of implications.
For an explicit scheme, $\theta_1$ = 1.0 and $\theta_2$ = 0.0. For
a fully implicit scheme, $\theta_1$ = 0.0 and $\theta_2$ = 1.0.
For a semi-implicit scheme $\theta_1$ = 0.5 and $\theta_2$ = 0.5.
The degree of implicitness is calculated by the code, based on the ratio
of the time-step size, $\Delta t$ , to a user-supplied time constant for
fuel-pin heat transfer, $\tau_{\text{ht}}$ . As explained in Section 3.19.1,
the expression used for $\theta_1$ and $\theta_2$ in TSHTRN are

$$\theta_{2} = \frac{1.65 + x}{3.3 + x}$$ [eq 3.3-11]

where

$$x = \frac{\Delta t}{\tau_{\text{ht}}}$$ [eq 3.3-12]

and

$$\theta_{1} = 1.0 - \theta_{2}$$ [eq 3.3-13]

Also, large relative changes in coolant flow rate, w, during a long heat
transfer time step can lead to anomalous coolant temperature changes if
the value of $\theta_1$ is too large; therefore, $\theta_2$ is
calculated as

$$\theta_{2} = \frac{\left| w_{1} \right|}{\left| w_{1} \right| + \left| w_{2} \right|}$$ [eq 3.3-14]

if Eq. (3.3-14) gives a larger value than Eq. (3.3-11). In Eq. (3.3-14),
 $w_1$ and $w_2$ are the coolant flow rates at the
beginning and end of the heat-transfer time step, respectively. In any
case, Eq. (3.3-13) is used for $\theta_1$ .

The heat transfer time step size is limited to a relatively small value
(0.02 s or less) after the onset of boiling, and so TSHTRV used
 $\theta_1$ = $\theta_2$ = 0.5.

The finite difference equations used for the fuel and for the two inner
cladding nodes are the same in TSHTRN and TSHTRV. The differences
between these routines start at the outer cladding node.

In general, the time derivative of a variable $y$ is approximated as

$$\frac{\partial \text{y}}{\partial \text{t}} = \frac{y\left( t + \Delta t \right) - y\left( t \right)}{\Delta t}$$ [eq 3.3-15]

and the spatial derivative is approximated as

$$\frac{\partial \text{y}}{\partial \text{z}} \simeq \theta_{1}\left\lbrack \frac{y\left( t,z + \Delta z \right) - y\left( t,z \right)}{\Delta z} \right\rbrack\ + \theta_{2}\left\lbrack \frac{y\left( t + \Delta t,z + \Delta z \right) - y\left( t + \Delta t,z \right)}{\Delta z} \right\rbrack$$ [eq 3.3-16]

In the following sections, it will be useful to refer to Figure 3.2.4
for the radial node structure and the definitions of the radial node
indexes.

[anchor: fuel-inner-surface-node-1]

#### 3.3.1.2.1. Fuel Inner Surface, Node 1

There is an adiabatic boundary at the fuel inner surface; so the node 1,
Eq. (3.3-1) becomes

$$\begin{split}m_{\text{f}}\left( 1 \right)\ \overline{c}_{\text{f}}\left( 1 \right)\left\lbrack \frac{T_{2}\left( 1 \right) - T_{1}\left( 1 \right)}{t} \right\rbrack \\
= \frac{2\pi r \left( 2 \right) \Delta z \left( j \right)\overline{k}_{1,2}}{\Delta r_{1,2}}\left\{ \theta_{1}\left\lbrack T_{1}\left( 2 \right) - T_{1}\left( 1 \right) \right\rbrack + \right. \\ \left.
\theta_{2}\left\lbrack T_{2}\left( 2 \right) - T_{2}\left( 1 \right) \right\rbrack \right\} + Q(1)\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-17]

where

 $m_f (i)$ = fuel mass at node i

 $\overline{c}\left( i \right)$ = fuel heat capacity at
radial node $i$ at $t = t_1 + \theta_2 \Delta t$ 

 $T_2 (i)$ = temperature at $t+\Delta t$ 

 $T_1 (i)$ = temperature at time $t$ 

 $\Delta z (j)$ = axial mesh height

$$\Delta r (1) = 2\left\lbrack r\left( 2 \right) - r\left( 1 \right) \right\rbrack$$ [eq 3.3-18]

$$\Delta r \left( i \right) = r\left( i + 1 \right) - r\left( i \right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ 2\ \ \leq \ \ i\ \ \leq \ \ \text{NN}$$ [eq 3.3-19]

$$\Delta r\left( \text{NT} \right) = 2\left\lbrack r\left( \text{NR} \right) - r\left( \text{NT} \right) \right\rbrack$$ [eq 3.3-20]

$$\Delta r\left( i \right) = \ \frac{\left\lbrack r\left( \text{NE}' \right) - r~(\text{NE}) \right\rbrack}{2}\ \ \ i = \text{NE}'',\ \text{NE},\ \text{NE}'$$ [eq 3.3-21]

(note: $\text{NE}''\ = \ \text{NR}$ )

$$\Delta r_{\text{i,i} + 1} = \frac{\Delta r\left( i \right) + \Delta r\left( i + 1 \right)}{2}\ \ 1\ \ \leq \ \ i\ \ \leq \ \ \text{NE}$$ [eq 3.3-22]

$$Q\left( i \right) = \frac{\overline{P}\left( j \right)\left( 1 - \gamma_{\text{e}} - \gamma_{\text{c}} - \gamma_{\text{s}} \right)\ P_{\text{r}}\left( i \right)m_{\text{f}}\left( i \right)}{\sum_{\text{ii} = 1}^{\text{NT}}{~ P_{\text{r}}\left( \text{ii} \right)m_{\text{f}}\left( \text{ii} \right)}}\ \ 1\ \ \leq \ \ i\ \ \leq \ \ \text{NT}$$ [eq 3.3-23]

$$Q\left( \text{NE}'' \right) = \frac{\overline{P}\left( j \right)\gamma_{\text{e}}}{4}$$ [eq 3.3-24]

$$Q\left( \text{NE} \right) = \frac{\overline{P}\left( j \right)\gamma_{\text{e}}}{2}$$ [eq 3.3-25]

$$Q\left( \text{NE}' \right) = \frac{\overline{P}\left( j \right)\gamma_{\text{e}}}{4} = Q\left( \text{NE}'' \right)$$ [eq 3.3-26]

where

 $\overline{P}\left( j \right)$ = total power (watts)
in axial node j

 $P_{\text{r}} (i)$ = radial power shape per unit mass

and

 $\gamma_{\text{e}},\gamma_{\text{c}},\gamma_{\text{s}}$ = fraction of power in direct
heating of clad, coolant, and structure, respectively.

The thermal conductivity, $\overline{k}_{1,2}$ used
in Eq. (3.3-17) is a weighted average of the values for the two adjacent
nodes. It is calculated as

$${\overline{k}}_{\text{i,i} + 1} = \frac{k\left( i \right)k\left( i + 1 \right) ~ \left\lbrack \Delta r \left( i \right) + \Delta r\left( i + 1 \right) \right\rbrack}{k\left( i \right) \Delta r \left( i + 1 \right) + k\left( i + 1 \right) \Delta r \left( i \right)}$$ [eq 3.3-27]

where

 $k(i)$ = thermal conductivity for radial node i, evaluated using the
fuel temperature extrapolated to $t + \theta_2 \Delta t$ 

Eq. (3.3-27) is carried over from SAS3D. The fuel restructuring
algorithm used in SAS3D uses up to three different fuel types (columnar,
equiaxed, and unrestructured) for the fuel at each axial node. Sharp
boundaries between fuel types are used. The radial mesh is adjusted, if
necessary, so that fuel-type boundaries fall on radial node boundaries.
One consequence of the sharp fuel-type boundaries in SAS3D is that the
fuel thermal conductivity can change significantly from one node to the
next at a fuel-type boundary. The average thermal conductivity of
Eq. (3.3-27) will give accurate steady-state fuel temperatures even if the
thermal conductivity has large jumps at node boundaries. The fuel
restructuring provided by DEFORM-IV in SAS4A is somewhat smoother than
that used in SAS3D, and the node-to-node changes in thermal conductivity
in SAS4A are usually smaller than the corresponding changes at fuel-type
boundaries in SAS3D, so the need for special weighting of the fuel
thermal conductivities in SAS4A Lis less than in SAS3D, but Eq. (3.3-27)
still provides more accurate fuel temperatures than simpler weighting
schemes.

Note that even though most of the variables in Eq. (3.3-17) to Eq. (3.3-27)
vary with axial node $j$ , the subscript $j$ has only been included for
some of these variables.

[anchor: inner-fuel-nodes-nodes-2-to-nn]

#### 3.3.1.2.2. Inner Fuel Nodes, Nodes 2 to NN

For fuel radial node I, Eq. (3.3-1) becomes

$$\begin{split} m_{\text{f}}\left( i \right)\overline{c}_{\text{f}}\left( i \right) ~ \frac{\left\lbrack T_{2}\left( i \right) - T_{1}\left( i \right) \right\rbrack}{\Delta t} \\
 = \frac{2\pi r\left( i + 1 \right) \Delta z \left( j \right){\overline{k}}_{\text{i,i} + 1}}{\Delta r_{\text{i,i} + 1}}\left\{ ~ \theta_{1} ~ \left\lbrack T_{1}\left( i + 1 \right) - T_{1}\left( i \right) \right\rbrack + \theta_{2} ~ \left\lbrack T_{2}\left( i + 1 \right) - T_{2}\left( i \right) \right\rbrack ~ \right\} \\
 - \frac{2\pi r\left( i \right) \Delta z \left( j \right){\overline{k}}_{\text{i} - 1,\text{i}}}{\Delta r_{\text{i} - 1,\text{i}}}\left\{ ~ \theta_{1} ~ \left\lbrack T_{1}\left( i \right) - T_{1}\left( i - 1 \right) \right\rbrack + \theta_{2} ~ \left\lbrack T_{2}\left( i \right) - T_{2}\left( i - 1 \right) \right\rbrack ~ \right\} + Q\left( i \right)\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-28]

Note that the left-hand side of Eq. (3.3-28) represents the change in
internal energy as node $i$ , whereas the terms on the right-hand side
represent heat conduction into node $i$ from nodes $i+1$ and $i-1$ , as
well as the heat source in node $i$ .

[anchor: fuel-outer-surface-node-node-nt]

#### 3.3.1.2.3. Fuel Outer Surface Node, Node NT

The heat flux $q_{\text{fc}}$ , from the fuel outer surface to the
cladding inner surface contains both a bond gap conductance and a
radiation term.

$$q_{\text{fc}} = h_{\text{b}}\left\lbrack \text{T}\left( \text{NT} \right) - \text{T}\left(\text{NE}'' \right) \right\rbrack + \varepsilon\sigma\ \ \left\lbrack \text{T}\left( \text{NT} \right)^{4} - \text{T}\left( \text{NE}'' \right)^{4} \right\rbrack$$ [eq 3.3-29]

where

 $h_{\text{b}}$ = bond conductance

 $\epsilon$ = thermal emissivity of the fuel

and

 $\sigma$ = Stefan-Boltzmann constant.

The $T^4$ terms are rewritten as

$$T\left( \text{NT} \right)^{4} - T\left( \text{NE}'' \right)^{4} = h_{\text{r}}\left\lbrack T\left( \text{NT} \right) - T\left( \text{NE}'' \right) \right\rbrack$$ [eq 3.3-30]

where

$$h_{\text{r}} = \frac{T_{1}\left( \text{NT} \right)^{4} - T_{1}\left( \text{NE}'' \right)^{4}}{T_{1}(\text{NT}) - T_{1}(\text{NE}'')} = \left\lbrack T_{1}\left( \text{NT} \right) + T_{1}\left( \text{NE}'' \right) \right\rbrack\left\lbrack T_{1}\left( \text{NT} \right)^{2} + T_{1}\left( \text{NE}'' \right)^{2} \right\rbrack$$ [eq 3.3-31]

The approximation is then made that $h_\text{r}$ is a constant for a
time step, and the equation for node NT becomes

$$\begin{split} m_{\text{f}}\left( \text{NT} \right){\overline{c}}_{\text{f}}\left( \text{NT} \right)\ \frac{T_{2}\left( \text{NT} \right) - T_{1}(\text{NT})}{\Delta t} = \frac{2\pi r\left( \text{NT} \right) \Delta z \left( j \right){\overline{k}}_{\text{NN,NT}}}{\Delta r_{\text{NN,NT}}} \left\{ \theta_{1}\left\lbrack T_{1}\left( \text{NN} \right) - T_{1}\left( \text{NT} \right) \right\rbrack + \right. \\ \left.
 \theta_{2}\left\lbrack T_{2}\left( \text{NN} \right) - T_{2}\left( \text{NT} \right) \right\rbrack \right\}\ + 2 \pi r \left( \text{NR} \right) \Delta z \left( j \right)\left\lbrack h_{\text{b}} + \epsilon\sigma h_{\text{r}} \right\rbrack\ \left\{ \theta_{1}\left\lbrack T_{1}\left( \text{NE}'' \right) - T_{1}(\text{NT}) \right\rbrack \right. \\ \left.
 + \theta_{2}\left\lbrack T_{2}\left( \text{NE}'' \right) - T_{2}(\text{NT}) \right\rbrack \right\} + Q\left( \text{NT} \right)\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-32]

[anchor: cladding-inner-node]

#### 3.3.1.2.4. Cladding Inner Node

For the cladding inner node, $\text{NE}''$ , Eq. (3.3-1) becomes

$$\begin{split} \frac{m_{\text{e}}c_{\text{e}}}{4}\frac{T_{2}\left( \text{NE}'' \right) - T_{1}\left( \text{NE}'' \right)}{\Delta t} \\
 = \frac{2\pi\Delta z\left( j \right){\overline{r}}_{\text{NE}}\ k_{\text{NE}'',\text{NE}}}{\Delta r_{\text{NE}'',\text{NE}}}\left\{ \theta_{1}\ \left\lbrack T_{1}\left( \text{NE} \right) - T_{1}\left( \text{NE}'' \right) \right\rbrack + \theta_{2}\ \left\lbrack T_{2}\left( \text{NE} \right) - T_{2}\left( \text{NE}'' \right) \right\rbrack \right\} \\
 - 2\pi r\left( \text{NR} \right) \Delta z \left( j \right)\left( h_{\text{b}} + \varepsilon\sigma h_{\text{r}} \right)\left\{ \theta_{1}\ \left\lbrack T_{1}\left( \text{NE}'' \right) - T_{1}\left( \text{NT} \right) \right\rbrack \right.\ \\
 + \theta_{2}\ \left. \ \left\lbrack T_{2}\left( \text{NE}'' \right) - T_{2}\left( \text{NT} \right) \right\rbrack\ \right\} + Q\left( \text{NE}'' \right)\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-33]

where

 $c_{\text{e}}$ = cladding heat capacity

$$m_{\text{e}} = \text{cladding mass} = 2\pi\rho_{\text{e}}\left\lbrack r\left( \mathrm{\text{NE}}' \right)^{2} - r\left( \mathrm{\text{NE}} \right)^{2} \right\rbrack \Delta z (j)$$ [eq 3.3-34]

 $p_\text{e}$ = cladding density

and

$${\overline{r}}_{\text{NE}} = r\ \left( \text{NE} \right) + \frac{1}{4}\left\lbrack r \ \left( \text{NE}' \right) - r\ \left( \text{NE} \right) \right\rbrack$$ [eq 3.3-35]

Note that the factors of 4 in Eq. (3.3-33) and Eq. (3.3-35) come about because
the inner cladding node represents one fourth of the thickness of the
cladding.

[anchor: cladding-mid-point-node]

#### 3.3.1.2.5. Cladding Mid-point Node

For the cladding mid-point node, $NE$ , Eq. (3.3-1) becomes

$$\begin{split} \frac{m_{\text{e}}c_{\text{e}}}{2}\frac{T_{2}\left( \text{NE} \right) - T_{1}(\text{NE})}{ \Delta t } \\
 = \frac{2\pi{\overline{r}}_{\text{NE}'} \Delta z (j){\overline{k}}_{\text{NE,NE}'}}{\Delta r_{\text{NE,NE}'}} \left\{ \theta_{1}\left\lbrack T_{1}\left( \text{NE}' \right) - T_{1}(\text{NE}) \right\rbrack + \theta_{2}\left\lbrack T_{2}\left( \text{NE}' \right) - T_{2}(\text{NE}) \right\rbrack \right\} \\
 - \frac{2\pi{\overline{r}}_{\text{NE}} \Delta z (j){\overline{k}}_{\text{NE}''\text{,NE}}}{\Delta r_{\text{NE}''\text{,NE}}} \left\{ \theta_{1}\left\lbrack T_{1}\left( \text{NE} \right) - T_{1}(\text{NE}'') \right\rbrack + \theta_{2}\left\lbrack T_{2}\left( \text{NE} \right) - T_{2}(\text{NE}'') \right \rbrack \right \} + Q(\text{NE})\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-36]

where

$${\overline{r}}_{\text{NE}'} = r\ \left( \text{NE} \right) + \frac{3}{4}\left\lbrack r \left( \text{NE}' \right) - r\ \left( \text{NE} \right) \right\rbrack$$ [eq 3.3-37]

[anchor: cladding-outer-node]

#### 3.3.1.2.6. Cladding Outer Node

The outer cladding node, $\text{NE}'$ , transfers heat to both the cladding mid-point node
and the coolant node, so the equation for the outer cladding node
temperature is

$$\begin{split} \frac{m_{\text{e}}c_{\text{e}}}{4}\frac{T_{2}\left( \text{NE}' \right) - T_{1}(\text{NE}')}{ \Delta t } \\
 = - \frac{2\pi{\overline{r}}_{\text{NE}'} \Delta z (j){\overline{k}}_{\text{NE,NE}'}}{{\overline{r}}_{\text{NE,NE}'}}\ \left\{ \theta_{1}\left\lbrack T_{1}\left( \text{NE}' \right) - T_{1}(\text{NE}) \right\rbrack + \theta_{2}\left\lbrack T_{2}\left( \text{NE}' \right) - T_{2}(\text{NE}) \right\rbrack \right\} \\
 + 2 \pi r \left( \text{NE}' \right) \Delta z (j)\ \left\{ \theta_{1}h_{\text{c}1}(j)\left\lbrack T_{1}\left( \text{NC} \right) - T_{1}\left( \text{NE}' \right) \right\rbrack + \theta_{2}h_{\text{c}2}(j)\left\lbrack T_{2}\left( \text{NC} \right) - T_{2} \left( \text{NE}' \right) \right \rbrack \right\} + Q(\text{NE}')\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-38]

Note that $T(\text{NC}) = {\overline{T}}_{c}\left( jc \right)$ .
Also, $hc_1$ and $hc_2$ are the coolant heat-transfer
coefficients at $t \text{ and } t + \Delta t$ as
calculated from Eq. (3.3-9),

[anchor: coolant-node-nc]

#### 3.3.1.2.7. Coolant, Node NC

Coolant temperatures are calculated for the whole length of the
subassembly, whereas fuel temperatures are computed in the core and
blankets only. Therefore, the axial coolant node mesh extends beyond the
fuel mesh; and the coolant axial node index, $jc$ , is related to the
fuel axial node index, $j$ , by

$$jc = j + j_{\text{cblbt}} - 1$$ [eq 3.3-39]

where $j_{\text{cblbt}}$ is the coolant node at the bottom of the lower
blanket. In the $T_1 (i,j)$ and $T_2 (i,j)$ arrays,
the coolant node corresponds to

$$T_{2}\left( \text{NC,j} \right) = \overline{T}_{\text{c}}\left( jc \right)$$ [eq 3.3-40]

In the non-voiding case, the coolant flow is usually upward. In such a
situation, the transient calculation for a time step starts at the
subassembly inlet and works upward through the lower reflector zones,
through the pin section, and finally through the upper reflector zones.
The code can also handle downward coolant flow during the transient,
although the initial steady-state coolant flows must all be upward. In
the downward situation, the calculation starts at the top of the
subassembly and works down to the inlet.

In the core and blanket regions, Eq. (3.3-5) becomes

$$\begin{split} {\overline{\rho}}_{\text{c}}\left( jc \right){\overline{c}}_{\text{c}}\left( jc \right)\frac{T_{2}\left( \text{NC} \right) - T_{1}\left( \text{NC} \right)}{\Delta t} + \frac{2{\overline{c}}_{\text{c}}\left( jc \right)}{\Delta z\left( j \right)A_{\text{c}}\left( jc \right)}\ \left\{ \theta_{1}\left| w_{1} \right| \left\lbrack T_{1}\left( \text{NC} \right) - {T'}_{1} \right\rbrack \right. \\ \left.
 + \theta_{2}\left| w_{2} \right| \left\lbrack T_{2}\ \left( \text{NC} \right) - {T'}_{2} \right\rbrack\ \right\} = \frac{Q\left( \text{NC} \right)}{A_{\text{c}}\left( jc \right)\Delta z\left( j \right)} + \frac{2\pi r\left( NE' \right)}{A_{\text{c}}\left( jc \right)} \\
 \left\{ \theta_{1}\ h_{\text{c}1}\left( j \right) \left\lbrack T_{1}\left( \text{NE}' \right) - T_{1}\left( \text{NC} \right) \right\rbrack + \theta_{2}h_{\text{c}2}\left( j \right)\left\lbrack T_{2}\ \left( NE' \right) - T_{2}\left( \text{NE} \right) \right\rbrack \right\} \\
 + \frac{S_{\text{pr}}}{A_{\text{c}}\left( jc \right)}\left\{ \theta_{1}\ H_{\text{sic}1}\left( jc \right) \left\lbrack T_{1}\left( \text{NSI} \right) - T_{1}\left( \text{NC} \right) \right\rbrack \right. \\ \left.
 + \theta_{2}\ H_{\text{sic}2}\left( jc \right) \left\lbrack T_{2}\left( \text{NSI} \right) - T_{2}\left( \text{NC} \right) \right\rbrack \right\}\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-41]

where

Spr = structure perimeter

w1 and w2 = the coolant mass flow rates (kg/s) at
t and $t + t$ 

NSI = the inner structure node

$$\begin{split} {T'}_{1} = \begin{cases}
T_{\text{c}1} \left( jc \right) & \text{if}\ w_{1} \geq 0 \\
T_{\text{c}} \left( jc + 1 \right) & \text{if}\ w_{1} < \ 0 \\
\end{cases}\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-42]

$$\begin{split} {T'}_{2} = \begin{cases}
T_{\text{c}2} \left( jc \right) & \text{if}\ w_{2} \geq 0 \\
T_{\text{c}} \left( jc + 1 \right) & \text{if}\ w_{2} < \ 0 \\
\end{cases}\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-43]

and

Hsic = the heat-transfer coefficient from the structure inner
node to the coolant

$$H_{\text{sic}} = \frac{2h_{\text{c}} k_{\text{si}}}{2k_{\text{si}} + h_{\text{c}} d_{\text{sti}}}$$ [eq 3.3-44]

Note that Eq. (3.3-44) is obtained by adding thermal resistance in series:

$$\frac{1}{H_{\text{sic}}} = \frac{1}{h_{\text{c}}} + \frac{d_{\text{sti}}}{2k_{\text{si}}}$$ [eq 3.3-45]

[anchor: structure-inner-node-node-nsi]

#### 3.3.1.2.8. Structure Inner Node, Node NSI

In the core and blanket regions,

$$\begin{split} \left( \rho c \right)_{\text{sti}}d_{\text{sti}}\frac{T_{2}\left( \text{NSI} \right) - T_{1}\left( \text{NSI} \right)}{\Delta t} = \theta_{1}H_{\text{sic}1}\left( jc \right)\left\lbrack T_{1} \left( \text{NC} \right) - T_{1}\left( \text{NSI} \right) \right\rbrack \\
 +\theta_{2}\ H_{sic2}\left( jc \right)\left\lbrack T_{2}\ \left( \text{NC} \right) - T_{2}\left( \text{NSI} \right) \right\rbrack \\
 + H_{\text{stio}}\left( jc \right) \left\{\theta_{1} \left\lbrack T_{1}\left( \text{NSO} \right) - T_{1}\left( \text{NSI} \right) \right\rbrack \right. \\ \left.
 + \theta_{2}\left\lbrack T_{2} \left( \text{NSO} \right) - T_{2}\left( \text{NSI} \right) \right\rbrack \right\} + Q_{\text{st}}\frac{d_{\text{sti}}}{d_{\text{sti}} + d_{\text{sto}}}\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-46]

where

NSO = outer structure node

 $d_{\text{sti}}$ = thickness of inner structure node

 $d_{\text{sto}}$ = thickness of outer structure node

$$H_{\text{stio}} = \frac{2k_{\text{si}}k_{\text{so}}}{d_{\text{sti}}k_{\text{so}} + d_{\text{sto}}k_{\text{si}}}$$ [eq 3.3-47]

 $k_{\text{si}}$ = thermal conductivity of the inner structure node

 $k_{\text{so}}$ = thermal conductivity of the outer structure node

and

 $Q_{\text{st}} = \frac{\gamma_{\text{s}}\overline{P}(j)}{S_{\text{pr}} \Delta z(j)}$ = direct heating source in the structure.

The left-hand side of Eq. (3.3-46) represents the change in internal
energy in the node. The terms on the right-hand side represent heat flow
from the coolant and the outer structure node, as well as direct heating
of the structure by neutrons and gamma rays. It is assumed that the
direct heating source is divided between the inner and outer nodes in
proportion to their thicknesses.

[anchor: structure-outer-node-node-nso]

#### 3.3.1.2.9. Structure Outer Node, Node NSO

In the core and blankets,

$$\begin{split}\left( \rho c \right)_{\text{sto}}d_{\text{sto}}\frac{T_{2}\left( \mathrm{\text{NSO}} \right) - T_{1}(\mathrm{\text{NSO}})}{ \Delta t } = H_{\text{stio}}\left( jc \right)\left\{ \theta_{1}\left\lbrack T_{1}\left( \mathrm{\text{NSI}} \right) - T_{1}\left( \mathrm{\text{NSO}} \right) \right\rbrack \right. \\ \left.
+ \theta_{2}\left\lbrack T_{2}\left( \mathrm{\text{NSI}} \right) - T_{2}(\mathrm{\text{NSO}}) \right\rbrack \right\} + Q_{\text{st}}\left( jc \right)\frac{d_{\text{sto}}}{d_{\text{sti}} + d_{\text{sto}}} + \frac{Q_{\text{chch}}(jc)}{S_{\text{pr}}}\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-48]

where

 $Q_{\text{chch}} = Q_{\text{chch},\text{I}}$ = subassembly-to-subassembly linear heat transfer rate.

The values used for $Q_{\text{chch}}$ are discussed in Section 3.11.

[anchor: solution-of-finite-difference-equations]

#### 3.3.1.2.10. Solution of Finite Difference Equations

Eq. (3.3-17), Eq. (3.3-28),
Eq. (3.3-32), Eq. (3.3-33),
Eq. (3.3-36), Eq. (3.3-38),
Eq. (3.3-41), Eq. (3.3-46),
and Eq. (3.3-48) can be written in matrix form, yielding a
tri-diagonal matrix of the form

$$\begin{split}\left\lbrack \begin{matrix}
\alpha_{1} + \beta_{1} \\
- \beta_{1} \\
0 \\
\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ . \\
\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ . \\
\\
\\
\\
\\
0 \\
0 \\
\end{matrix}\begin{matrix}
\\
\\
\\
\\
\\
. \\
\ \ \ \ \ . \\
\\
\\
\\
\\
\end{matrix}\begin{matrix}
- \beta_{1} \\
\alpha_{2} + \beta_{1} + \beta_{2} \\
- \beta_{2} \\
\\
\\
\\
\\
.\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \\
.\ \ \ \ \ \\
...0 \\
.... \\
\end{matrix}\begin{matrix}
0 \\
- \beta_{2} \\
\alpha_{3} + \beta_{2} + \beta_{3} \\
\\
\\
\\
\\
\\
\\
- \beta_{\mathrm{\text{NC}}} \\
0 \\
\end{matrix}\begin{matrix}
0 \\
0 \\
- \beta_{2} \\
\\
\\
\\
\\
\\
\\
\alpha_{\mathrm{\text{NSI}}} + \beta_{\mathrm{\text{NC}}} + \beta_{\mathrm{\text{NSI}}} \\
- \beta_{\mathrm{\text{NSI}}} \\
\end{matrix}\begin{matrix}
\ldots \\
\ldots \\
\ldots \\
\\
\\
\\
\\
\\
\\
- \beta_{\mathrm{\text{NSI}}} \\
\alpha_{\mathrm{\text{NSO}}} + \beta_{\mathrm{\text{NSI}}} + \beta_{\mathrm{\text{NSO}}} \\
\end{matrix} \right\rbrack\ \begin{bmatrix}
T_{2}(1) \\
T_{2}(2) \\
T_{2}(3) \\
\\
\\
\\
\\
\\
\\
T_{2}(\mathrm{\text{NSI}}) \\
T_{2}(\mathrm{\text{NSO}}) \\
\end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix}
D_{1} \\
D_{2} \\
D_{3} \\
\\
\\
\\
\\
\\
\\
D_{\mathrm{\text{NSI}}} \\
D_{\mathrm{\text{NSO}}}
\end{bmatrix}\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-49]

where

$$\alpha_{1} = \frac{M_{\text{f}}\left( i \right){\overline{c}}_{\text{f}}\left( i \right)}{2\pi\Delta z\left( j \right)}\ \ \ \ \ \text{for}\ \ i\ \ = \ \ 1,...\ \ \text{NT}$$ [eq 3.3-50]

$$\alpha_{\text{NE}''} = \frac{M_{\text{e}}c_{\text{e}}}{8\pi\Delta z \left( j \right)}$$ [eq 3.3-51]

$$\alpha_{\text{NE}} = \frac{M_{\text{e}}c_{\text{e}}}{4\pi\Delta z\left( j \right)}\ = 2\alpha_{\text{NE}''}$$ [eq 3.3-52]

$$\alpha_{\text{NE}'} = \alpha_{\text{NE}''}$$ [eq 3.3-53]

$$\alpha_{\text{NC}} = \frac{{\overline{\rho}}_{\text{c}}\left( jc \right){\overline{c}}_{\text{c}}\left( jc \right)A_{\text{c}}\left( jc \right)}{2\pi} + \frac{{\overline{c}}_{\text{c}}\left( jc \right)}{\pi \Delta z\left( jc \right)}\theta_{2} | w_{2} | \Delta t$$ [eq 3.3-54]

$$\alpha_{\text{NSI}} = \frac{\left( \rho c \right)_{\text{sti}}d_{\text{sti}}S_{\text{pr}}}{2\pi}$$ [eq 3.3-55]

$$\alpha_{\text{NSO}} = \frac{\left( \rho c \right)_{\text{sto}}d_{\text{sto}}S_{\text{pr}}}{2\pi}$$ [eq 3.3-56]

$$\beta_{\text{i}} = \frac{r\left( i + 1 \right){\overline{k}}_{\text{i,i} + 1}}{\Delta r_{\text{i,i} + 1}}\theta_{2}\ \Delta t\ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for}\ \ i\ \ = \ \ 1,....NN$$ [eq 3.3-57]

$$\beta_{\text{NT}} = r\left( \text{NR} \right)\theta_{2}\ \left\lbrack h_{b} + \varepsilon\sigma h_{\text{r}} \right\rbrack\Delta t$$ [eq 3.3-58]

$$\beta_{\text{NE}''} = \frac{\overline{r}_{\text{NE}}\ \overline{k}_{\text{NE}''\text{,NE}}\ }{\Delta r_{\text{NE}''\text{,NE}}}\theta_{2}\Delta t$$ [eq 3.3-59]

$$\beta_{\text{NE}} = \frac{\overline{r}_{\text{NE}'}\ \overline{k}_{\text{NE,NE}'}\ }{\Delta r_{\text{NE,NE}'}}\theta_{2} \Delta t$$ [eq 3.3-60]

$$\beta_{\text{NE}'} = r_{\text{NE}'}\ h_{c2}\left( j \right)\theta_{2} \Delta t$$ [eq 3.3-61]

$$\beta_{\text{NC}} = \frac{S_{\text{pr}}\ }{2\ \pi}H_{\text{sic2}}\left( jc \right)\ \theta_{2} \Delta t$$ [eq 3.3-62]

$$\beta_{\text{NSI}} = \frac{S_{\text{pr}}\ }{2\ \pi}H_{\text{stio}}\left( jc \right)\ \theta_{2} \Delta t$$ [eq 3.3-63]

$$\beta_{\text{NSO}} = 0$$ [eq 3.3-64]

$$D_{1} = T_{1}\left( 1 \right)\left\lbrack \alpha_{1} - \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}\beta_{1} \right\rbrack + \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}\beta_{1}T_{1}\left( 2 \right) + \psi_{1}$$ [eq 3.3-65]

$$\begin{split}D_{\text{i}} = \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}\beta_{\text{i} - 1}T_{1}\left( i - 1 \right) + T_{1}\left( i \right)\left\lbrack \alpha_{1} - \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}\left( \beta_{\text{i} - 1} + \beta_{\text{i}} \right) \right\rbrack \\
+ \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}\beta_{\text{i}}T_{1}\left( i + 1 \right) + \psi_{1} \quad \text{for}\ i = 2,...,NSI\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-66]

$$D_{\text{NSO}} = \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}\beta_{\text{NSI}}T_{1}\left( \text{NSI} \right) + T_{1}\left( \text{NSO} \right)\left\lbrack \alpha_{\text{NSO}} - \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}\left( \beta_{\text{NSI}} + \beta_{\text{NSO}} \right) \right\rbrack + \psi_{\text{NSO}}$$ [eq 3.3-67]

$$\psi_{\text{i}} = \frac{Q\left( i \right) \Delta t }{2\pi\Delta z\left( j \right)}\ \ \text{for}\ \ i = 1,...,\ \ \text{NE}'$$ [eq 3.3-68]

$$\psi_{\text{NC}} = \frac{Q\left( \text{NC} \right) \Delta t }{2\pi\Delta z\left( j \right)}\ + \frac{{\overline{c}}_{\text{c}}\left( jc \right)\Delta t}{\pi \Delta z\left( j \right)}\left\lbrack \theta_{1}\left| w_{1} \right|{T'}_{1} + \theta_{2}\left| w_{2} \right|{T'}_{2} \right\rbrack$$ [eq 3.3-69]

$$\psi_{\text{NSI}} = \frac{Q\left( \mathrm{\text{NSI}} \right) \Delta t }{2\pi\Delta z(j)}$$ [eq 3.3-70]

and

$$\psi_{\text{NSO}} = \frac{Q\left( \text{NSO} \right) \Delta t }{2\pi\Delta z\left( j \right)} + \frac{1}{2\pi}Q_{\text{chch}}\Delta t$$ [eq 3.3-71]

In these equations the $\alpha$ array is related to heat capacity, the $\beta$ 
array is related to heat transfer between adjacent nodes, and the $\psi$ 
array is related to the heat source.

The matrix equations 3.3-49 are solved by Gaussian elimination. First
arrays $A_i$ and $S_i$ are defined:

$$A_{\text{i}} = \alpha_{\text{i}} + \beta_{\text{i}} + \beta_{\text{i} = 1} - \frac{\beta_{\text{i} - 1}^{2}}{A_{\text{i} - 1}}\ \ \ \ \ \text{for}\ \ i = 2,...,\ \ NSO$$ [eq 3.3-72]

$$S_{1} = D_{1}$$ [eq 3.3-73]

and

$$S_{\text{i}} = D_{\text{i}} + \frac{\beta_{\text{i} - 1}S_{\text{i} - 1}}{A_{\text{i} - 1}}\ \ \ \ \ \text{for}\ \ i = 2,...,\ \ NSO$$ [eq 3.3-74]

Then,

$$T_{2}\left( \text{NSO} \right) = \frac{S_{\text{NSO}}}{A_{\text{NSO}}}$$ [eq 3.3-75]

and

$$T_{2}\left( i \right) = S_{\text{i}} + \frac{\beta_{\text{i}}}{A_{\text{i}}}T_{2}\left( i + 1 \right)\ \ \ \ \text{for}\ \ i = NSI,\ \ NC,...,\ \ 1$$ [eq 3.3-76]

[anchor: reflector-zones]

## 3.3.2. Reflector Zones

In reflector zones, a two-node slab geometry treatment is used at each
axial node for heat transfer to the “reflector”. The reflector
represents any material in the subassembly outside the pin section.

Typically, this material includes shield orifice blocks near the
subassembly inlet, and instrumentation in the upper part of the
subassembly. Usually, this material does not come to the form of either
pure slabs or pure cylinders, and so any simple geometrical treatment of
it will be only an approximation. The best that one is likely to do with
a simple heat-transfer calculation is to use a slab calculation with
parameters chosen to match total heat capacity, total heat-transfer
surface area, and the approximate effective thickness of the material.

[anchor: section-3-3-2-1]

### 3.3.2.1. Basic Equations

The basic equations used for coolant and structure temperatures in
reflector zones are the same as Eq. (3.3-5) and Eq. (3.3-10) used in the core
and blanket regions, except that no heat generation is considered in the
reflector zones. Therefore, the $Q_{\text{c}}$ , term of Eq. (3.3-5)
and the $Q$ term of Eq. (3.3-10) are eliminated in the reflector
zones. The reflector is treated with a one-dimensional heat conduction
equation that is the same as the one used for the structure, except that
the thermal properties $\rho$ , $c$ , and $k$ used for the reflector can be
different from those used for the structure.

[anchor: section-3-3-2-2]

### 3.3.2.2. Finite Difference Equations

Figure 3.2.6 shows the radial mesh structures used for an axial node in
a reflector region.

[anchor: reflector-inner-node]

#### 3.3.2.2.1. Reflector Inner Node

Eq. (3.3-10) becomes

$$\begin{split}\left( \rho c \right)_{\text{r}}d_{\text{ri}}\frac{T_{\text{ri}2}\left( jc \right) - T_{\text{ri}1}\left( jc \right)}{\Delta t} = H_{\text{rio}}\left( jc \right)\ \left\{ \theta_{1}\ \left\lbrack T_{\text{ro}1}\left( jc \right) - T_{\text{ri}1}\left( jc \right) \right\rbrack \right. \\
\left. + \theta_{2}\left\lbrack T_{\text{ro}2}\left( jc \right) - T_{\text{ri}2}\left( jc \right) \right\rbrack \right\}\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-77]

where

 $\rho c_{\text{r}}$ = density times specific heat of the reflector

 $d_{\text{ri}}$ = thickness of inner node

 $T_{\text{ri}1},~ T_{\text{ri}2}$ = reflector inner
node temperature at the beginning and end of the time step

 $T_{\text{ro}},~ T_{\text{ro}2}$ = reflector outer
node temperature at the beginning and end of the t ime step

 $d_{\text{ro}}$ = thickness of outer reflector node

and

$$H_{\text{rio}} = \frac{2k_{\text{r}}}{d_{\text{ri}} + d_{\text{ro}}}$$ [eq 3.3-78]

Eq. (3.3-78) is obtained by adding thermal resistances in series:

$$\frac{1}{H_{\text{rio}}} = \frac{d_{\text{ri}}}{2k_{\text{r}}} + \frac{d_{\text{ro}}}{2k_{\text{r}}}$$ [eq 3.3-79]

Eq. (3.3-77) is similar to Eq. (3.3-48) for the outer structure node,
except there is no direct heating source in the reflectors.

[anchor: reflector-outer-node]

#### 3.3.2.2.2. Reflector Outer Node

The equation for the reflector outer node temperature is similar to
Eq. (3.3-46) for the structure inner node:

$$\begin{split}\left( \rho c \right)_{\text{r}}d_{\text{ro}}\frac{T_{\text{ro}2}\left( jc \right) - T_{\text{ro}1}\left( jc \right)}{\Delta t} \\
= \theta_{1}H_{\text{erc}1}\left( jc \right)\left\lbrack {\overline{T}}_{\text{c}1}\left( jc \right) - T_{\text{ro}1}\left( jc \right) \right\rbrack + \theta_{2}H_{\text{erc}2}\left( jc \right)\left\lbrack {\overline{T}}_{\text{c}2}\left( jc \right) - T_{\text{ro}2}\left( jc \right) \right\rbrack \\
+ H_{\text{rio}}\left( jc \right)\left\{ \theta_{1}\left\lbrack T_{\text{ri}1}\left( jc \right) - T_{\text{ro}1}\left( jc \right) \right\rbrack + \theta_{2}\left\lbrack T_{\text{ri}2}\left( jc \right) - T_{\text{ro}2}\left( jc \right) \right\rbrack \right\}\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-80]

where

$$H_{\text{erc}} = \frac{h_{\text{c}}k_{\text{r}}}{k_{\text{r}} + \frac{h_{\text{c}}d_{\text{ro}}}{2}}$$ [eq 3.3-81]

Note that

$${\overline{T}}_{\text{c}}\left( jc \right) = \left\lbrack \ T_{\text{c}}\left( jc \right) + T_{\text{c}}\left( jc + 1 \right) \right\rbrack\ /\ 2$$ [eq 3.3-82]

[anchor: coolant-node]

#### 3.3.2.2.3. Coolant Node

In the reflector zones and in the gas plenum, Eq. (3.3-5) becomes

$$\begin{split}{\overline{\rho}}_{\text{c}}\left( jc \right){\overline{c}}_{\text{c}}\left( jc \right)\frac{{\overline{T}}_{\text{c}2}\left( jc \right) - T_{\text{c}1}\left( jc \right)}{\Delta t} + \frac{2{\overline{c}}_{\text{c}}\left( jc \right)}{\Delta z\left( jc \right)A_{\text{c}}\left( jc \right)}\left\{ \theta_{1}\left| w_{1} \right|\left\lbrack {\overline{T}}_{\text{c}}\left( jc \right) - {T'}_{1} \right\rbrack \right. \\
\left. + \theta_{2}\left| w_{2} \right|\left\lbrack {\overline{T}}_{\text{c}2}\left( jc \right) - {T'}_{2} \right\rbrack\ \right\} \\
= \ \frac{S_{\text{er}}\left( \text{KZ} \right)}{A_{\text{c}}\left( jc \right)}\left\{ \theta_{1}H_{\text{erc}1}\left\lbrack T_{\text{er}1}\left( jc \right) - T_{\text{c}1}\left( jc \right)\ \right\rbrack \right. \\
\left. + \theta_{2}H_{\text{erc}2}\left\lbrack T_{\text{er}2}\left( jc \right) - {\overline{T}}_{\text{c}2}\left( jc \right) \right\rbrack\ \right\} + \frac{S_{\text{pr}}}{A_{\text{c}}\left( jc \right)}\ \left\{ \theta_{1}H_{\text{sic}1}\left\lbrack T_{\text{ri}1}\left( jc \right) \right.\ \right. \\
\left. \ \left. \ - T_{\text{c}1}\left( jc \right) \right\rbrack + \theta_{2}H_{\text{sci}2}\left( jc \right)\left\lbrack T_{\text{sti}2}\left( jc \right) - {\overline{T}}_{\text{c}2}\left( jc \right) \right\rbrack \right\}\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-83]

where, in reflector zones,

 $T_{\text{er}1}$ = $T_{\text{ro}1}$ 

 $T_{\text{er}2}$ = $T_{\text{ro}2}$ 

 $S_{\text{er}}$ = $S_{\text{r}}$ = reflector perimeter

 $S_{\text{pr}}$ = structure perimeter

and

 $T_{\text{sti}}$ = structure inner node temperature

[anchor: structure-nodes]

#### 3.3.2.2.4. Structure Nodes

The finite difference approximation to Eq. (3.3-10) for the structure
nodes is the same as in the core and blanket regions. Eq. (3.3-46)
and Eq. (3.3-48) are used, except that $Q_{\text{st}}$ is zero outside
the core and blankets.

[anchor: section-3-3-2-3]

### 3.3.2.3. Solution of Finite Difference Equations

$$\begin{split}\begin{bmatrix}
\alpha_{1} + \beta_{1} & - \beta_{1} & 0 & 0 & 0 \\
- \beta_{1} & \alpha_{2} + \beta_{1} + \beta_{2} & - \beta_{2} & 0 & 0 \\
0 & - \beta_{2} & \alpha_{3} + \beta_{2} + \beta_{3} & - \beta_{3} & 0 \\
0 & 0 & - \beta_{3} & \alpha_{4} + \beta_{3} + \beta_{4} & - \beta_{4} \\
0 & 0 & 0 & - \beta_{4} & \alpha_{5} + \beta_{4} + \beta_{5} \\
\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}
T_{ri2} \\
T_{ro2} \\
{\overline{T}}_{c2} \\
T_{Sti2} \\
T_{St02} \\
\end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix}
D_{1} \\
D_{2} \\
D_{3} \\
D_{4} \\
D_{5} \\
\end{bmatrix}\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-84]

where

$$\alpha_{1} = \left( \rho c \right)_{\text{r}}d_{\text{ri}}S_{\text{er}}\left( \text{KZ} \right)$$ [eq 3.3-85]

$$\alpha_{2} = \left( \rho c \right)_{\text{r}}d_{\text{ro}}S_{\text{er}}\left( \text{KZ} \right)$$ [eq 3.3-86]

$$\alpha_{3} = {\overline{\rho}}_{\text{c}}\ {\overline{c}}_{e}\ A_{\text{c}} + 2\ \ {\overline{c}}_{\text{c}}\ \theta_{2}\left| w_{2} \right| \Delta t$$ [eq 3.3-87]

$$\alpha_{4} = \left( \rho c \right)_{\text{sti}}d_{\text{sti}}S_{\text{pr}}$$ [eq 3.3-88]

$$\alpha_{5} = \left( \rho c \right)_{\text{sto}}d_{\text{sto}}S_{\text{pr}}$$ [eq 3.3-89]

$$\beta_{1} = \theta_{2}H_{\text{rio}}\left( jc \right)S_{\text{er}} \Delta t$$ [eq 3.3-90]

$$\beta_{2} = \theta_{2}H_{\text{erc}2}\left( jc \right)S_{\text{er}} \Delta t$$ [eq 3.3-91]

$$\beta_{3} = \theta_{2}H_{\text{sic}2}\left( jc \right)S_{\text{pr}} \Delta t$$ [eq 3.3-92]

$$\beta_{4} = \theta_{2}H_{\text{stio}}\left( jc \right)S_{\text{pr}} \Delta t$$ [eq 3.3-93]

$$\beta_{5} = \theta$$ [eq 3.3-94]

$$D_{1} = T_{\text{ri}1}\ \left\lbrack \alpha_{1} - \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}} \beta_{1} \right\rbrack + \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}\ \beta_{1}\ T_{\text{ro}1}$$ [eq 3.3-95]

$$\begin{split}D_{\text{i}} = \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}\ \beta_{\text{i} - 1}{T''}_{1}\left( \text{i} - 1 \right) + {T''}_{1}\left( i \right) \left\lbrack \alpha_{1} - \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}} \left( \beta_{\text{i} - 1} + \beta_{1} \right) \right\rbrack \\
+ \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}} \beta_{\text{i}}{T''}_{1}\left( i + 1 \right) \quad \text{for}\ i = 2,4\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-96]

$$\begin{split}D_{3} = \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}} \beta_{2}{T''}_{1}\left( 2 \right) + {T''}_{1}\left( 3 \right) \left\lbrack \alpha_{3} - \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}} \left( \beta_{2} + \beta_{3} \right) \right\rbrack + \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}} \beta_{3}{T''}_{1}\left( 4 \right) \\
+ 2 \frac{{\overline{c}}_{c}\Delta t}{\Delta z} \left\lbrack \theta_{1}\left| w_{1} \right|{T'}_{1} + \theta_{2}\left| w_{2} \right| {T'}_{2} \right\rbrack\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-97]

$$D_{5} = \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}} \beta_{4}{T''}_{1}\left( 4 \right) + {T''}_{1}\left( 5 \right) \left\lbrack \alpha_{5} - \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}} \left( \beta_{4} + \beta_{5} \right) \right\rbrack + S_{\text{pr}}\ Q_{\text{chch}}\Delta t$$ [eq 3.3-98]

$${T''}_{1}\left( 1 \right)\ \ = \ \ T_{\text{ri}1}\left( jc \right)$$ [eq 3.3-99]

$${T''}_{1}\left( 2 \right)\ \ = \ \ T_{\text{ro}1}\left( jc \right)$$ [eq 3.3-100]

$${T''}_{1}\left( 3 \right)\ \ = \ \ {\overline{T}}_{\text{c}1}\left( jc \right)$$ [eq 3.3-101]

$${T''}_{1}\left( 4 \right)\ \ = \ \ T_{\text{sti}1}\left( jc \right)$$ [eq 3.3-102]

and

$${T''}_{1}\left( 5 \right)\ \ = \ \ T_{\text{sto}1}\left( jc \right)$$ [eq 3.3-103]

This tri-diagonal matrix is solved in the same manner as in Section 3.3.1.2 above.

[anchor: gas-plenum-region]

## 3.3.3. Gas Plenum Region

In the gas plenum region, a single gas node is in contact with all axial
cladding nodes. A single radial node is used in the cladding, as
indicated in Figure 3.2.5.

[anchor: section-3-3-3-1]

### 3.3.3.1. Basic Equations

The basic equations used in the cladding, coolant, and structure in the
gas plenum region are the same as those used in the core and blankets,
except that there is no heat source term outside of the core and
blankets. The gas is assumed to transfer heat only to the cladding, and
the basic equation used for the gas temperature is

$$\left( z_{\text{pt}} - z_{\text{pb}} \right) (\rho c)_{\text{g}} A_{\text{g}} \frac{\text{dT}_{\text{g}}}{\text{dt}} = 2 \pi r_{\text{brp}} H_{\text{eg}} \int_{z_{\text{pb}}}^{z_{\text{pt}}} \left( T_{\text{e}} - T_{\text{g}} \right) \text{dz}$$ [eq 3.3-104]

where

 $z_{\text{pb}}$ = elevation at the bottom of the gas plenum

 $z_{\text{pt}}$ = elevation at the top of the gas plenum

 $(\rho c)_{\text{g}}$ = density times specific heat for the plenum gas

 $A_{\text{g}}$ = cross sectional area of the gas plenum

$$A_{\text{g}} = \pi r^2_{\text{brp}}$$ [eq 3.3-105]

 $r_{\text{brp}}$ = cladding inner radius in the gas plenum region

 $T_{\text{g}}$ = gas temperature

 $H_{\text{eg}}$ = heat-transfer coefficient from the plenum gas to the
cladding node

 $T_{\text{e}}$ = cladding temperature

[anchor: section-3-3-3-2]

### 3.3.3.2. Finite Difference Equations

[anchor: gas-plenum]

#### 3.3.3.2.1. Gas Plenum

Eq. (3.3-105) becomes

$$\begin{split}\left( \rho c \right)_{\text{g}}A_{\text{g}}\frac{\left( T_{\text{g}2} - T_{\text{g}1} \right)}{\Delta t} \\
= 2\pi r_{\text{brp}}H_{\text{eg}}\frac{\sum_{\text{jp}}\left\{ \theta_{1}\left\lbrack T_{\text{e}1}\left( jp \right) - T_{\text{g}1} \right\rbrack + \theta_{2}\left\lbrack T_{\text{e}2}\left( jp \right) - T_{\text{g}2} \right\rbrack \right\}\Delta z\left( jp \right)}{\sum_{\text{jp}}{\Delta z\left( jp \right)}}\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-106]

where

 $jp$ = plenum node

 $T_{\text{g}1}$ = plenum gas temperature at the beginning of the
time step

 $T_{\text{g}2}$ = plenum gas temperature at the end of the time
step

$$H_{\text{eg}} = \frac{1}{R_{\text{g}} + \frac{r_{\text{erp}} - r_{\text{brp}}}{2k_{\text{ep}}}} = \frac{2k_{\text{ep}}}{r_{\text{erp}} - r_{\text{brp}} + 2k_{\text{ep}}R_{\text{g}}}$$ [eq 3.3-107]

 $k_{\text{ep}}$ = cladding thermal conductivity in the gas plenum

and

 $R_{\text{g}}$ = thermal resistance of the gas.

[anchor: cladding-node]

#### 3.3.3.2.2. Cladding Node

The equation for the cladding node is

$$\begin{split}\rho_{\text{e}}c_{\text{e}}A_{\text{ep}}\frac{T_{\text{e}2}\left( jp \right) - T_{\text{e}1}\left( jp \right)}{\Delta t} \\
= 2\pi r_{\text{erp}}\left\{\theta_{1} \ H_{\text{erc}1}\left\lbrack {\overline{T}}_{\text{c}1}\left( jc \right) - T_{\text{e}1}\left( jp \right) \right\rbrack \right. \\ \left.
+ \theta_{2}H_{\text{erc}2}\left\lbrack {\overline{T}}_{\text{c}2}\left( jc \right) - T_{\text{e}2}\left( jp \right) \right\rbrack \right\} + \ 2\pi r_{\text{brp}}H_{\text{eg}}\left\{ \theta_{1} \left\lbrack T_{\text{g}1} - T_{\text{e}1}\left( jp \right) \right\rbrack \right. \\ \left.
+ \theta_{2} \left\lbrack T_{\text{g}2} - T_{\text{e}2}\left( jp \right) \right\rbrack \right\}\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-108]

where

 $\rho_{\text{e}}$ = cladding density

 $c_{\text{e}}$ = cladding specific heat

$$A_{\text{ep}} = \pi\left\lbrack r_{\text{erp}}^{2} - r_{\text{brp}}^{2}\ \right\rbrack$$ [eq 3.3-109]

and

 $r_{\text{erp}}$ = cladding outer radius in the gas plenum region

[anchor: section-3-3-3-2-3]

#### 3.3.3.2.3. Coolant Node

The equation for the coolant node in the gas plenum region is the same
as Eq. (3.3-83) for reflector zones, except that in the gas plenum

$$H_{\text{erc}} = \frac{h_{\text{e}}k_{\text{e}}}{k_{\text{e}} + \frac{h_{\text{c}}\left( r_{\text{erp}} - r_{\text{brp}} \right)}{2}}$$ [eq 3.3-110]

$$S_{\text{cr}} = 2\pi r_{\text{erp}}$$ [eq 3.3-111]

and

 $k_{\text{e}}$ = cladding thermal conductivity.

[anchor: section-3-3-3-2-4]

#### 3.3.3.2.4. Structure Nodes

The finite difference approximations used for the structure in the gas
plenum region are the same as those used in the core and blankets.
Eq. (3.3-46) and Eq. (3.3-48) are used, except that $Q_{\text{st}}$ 
is zero.

[anchor: section-3-3-3-3]

### 3.3.3.3. Solution of Finite Difference Equations

A direct solution of the finite difference equations would be
complicated, since Eq. (3.3-106) connects a number of axial nodes, each
containing a number of radial nodes. Instead, an approximate solution
method is used. This method uses the assumption that the total heat
capacity of the gas is much less than the total heat capacity of the
cladding in the gas plenum region, or

$$\left( \rho c \right)_{\text{g}}\ A_{\text{g}}\ < \ \ < \ \ \rho_{\text{e}}\ c_{\text{e}}\ A_{\text{ep}}$$ [eq 3.3-112]

which should always be the case.

The solution method contains five steps:

Set $T_{\text{g}2}$ = $T_{\text{g}1}$ .

Solve Eq. (3.3-108), Eq. (3.3-83), Eq. (3.3-46), and Eq. (3.3-48) for all axial nodes in
the gas plenum to get new cladding, coolant, and structure
temperatures.

Use Eq. (3.3-106) to obtain a new computed value for
 $T_{\text{g}2}$ .

For each axial node, calculate the heat flow error, $\Delta E$ , due to
the assumption in step 1:

$$\Delta E = \left( \rho c \right)_{\text{g}}\ A_{\text{g}}\ \left( T_{\text{g}2} - T_{\text{g}1} \right)$$ [eq 3.3-113]

Add this heat flow to the cladding, changing the cladding
temperature:

$$T_{\text{e}2} = T_{\text{c}2} + \frac{\Delta E}{\rho_{\text{e}}c_{\text{e}}A_{\text{ep}}}$$ [eq 3.3-114]

In step 2, the equations for each axial node give a matrix equation of
the form

$$\begin{split} \begin{bmatrix}
\alpha_{1} + \beta_{1} & - \beta_{1} & 0 & 0 \\
- \beta_{1} & \alpha_{1} + \beta_{1} + \beta_{2} & - \beta_{2} & 0 \\
0 & - \beta_{2} & \alpha_{3} + \beta_{2} + \beta_{3} & - \beta_{3} \\
0 & 0 & - \beta_{3} & \alpha_{4} + \beta_{3} + \beta_{4} \\
\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}
{T''}_{2}\left( 1 \right) \\
{T''}_{2}\left( 2 \right) \\
{T''}_{2}\left( 3 \right) \\
{T''}_{2}\left( 4 \right) \\
\end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix}
D_{1} \\
D_{2} \\
D_{3} \\
D_{4}
\end{bmatrix}\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-115]

where

$$T_{2}'' \left( 1 \right) = T_{\text{e}2}(jp)$$ [eq 3.3-116]

$$T_{2}'' \left( 2 \right) = {\overline{T}}_{c2}(jc)$$ [eq 3.3-117]

$$T_{2}'' \left( 3 \right) = T_{\text{sti}2}(jc)$$ [eq 3.3-118]

$$T_{2}'' \left( 4 \right) = T_{\text{sto}2}(jc)$$ [eq 3.3-119]

$$\alpha_{1} = \rho_{\text{e}} c_{\text{e}} A_{\text{ep}} + 2\pi r_{\text{brp}} \Delta t \theta_{2}\ H_{\text{eg}}$$ [eq 3.3-120]

$$\alpha_{2} = {\overline{\rho}}_{\text{e}}{\overline{c}}_{\text{e}}A_{\text{e}} + 2{\overline{c}}_{c}\theta_{2}\ \left| w_{2} \right| \Delta t$$ [eq 3.3-121]

$$\alpha_{3} = \left( \rho c \right)_{\text{sti}}\ d_{\text{sti}}\ S_{\text{pr}}$$ [eq 3.3-122]

$$\alpha_{4} = \left( \rho c \right)_{\text{sto}}d_{\text{sto}}\ S_{\text{pr}}$$ [eq 3.3-123]

$$\beta_{1} = 2\pi\ r_{\text{erp}}\theta_{2}\ H_{\text{erc}2}\ \left( jc \right) \Delta t$$ [eq 3.3-124]

$$\beta_{2} = \theta_{2}\ H_{\text{sic}2}\ \left( jc \right)S_{\text{pr}} \Delta t$$ [eq 3.3-125]

$$\beta_{3} = \theta_{2}\ H_{\text{stio}2}\ \left( jc \right)S_{\text{pr}} \Delta t$$ [eq 3.3-126]

$$\beta_{4} = 0$$ [eq 3.3-127]

$$D_{1} = {T''}_{1}\left( 1 \right)\ \left\lbrack \alpha_{1} - \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}\ \beta_{1} \right\rbrack + \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}\ \beta_{1}\ {T''}_{1}\left( 2 \right) + \ 2\pi r_{\text{brp}}\Delta t H_{\text{eg}}\left\lbrack T_{g1} - T_{e1}\left( jp \right) \right\rbrack$$ [eq 3.3-128]

$$\begin{split}D_{2} = \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}\beta_{1}\ {T''}_{1}\left( 1 \right) + {T''}_{1}\ \left( 2 \right)\ \left\lbrack \alpha_{2} - \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}\ \left( \beta_{1} + \beta_{2} \right) \right\rbrack + \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}\ \beta_{2}\ {T''}_{1}\left( 3 \right) \\
+ 2\frac{{\overline{c}}_{c} \Delta t }{ \Delta z }\left\lbrack \theta_{1}\ \left| w_{1} \right|{T'}_{1} + \theta_{2}\left| w_{2} \right|{T'}_{2}\ \right\rbrack\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-129]

$$D_{3} = \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}\beta_{2}\ {T''}_{1}\left( 2 \right) + {T''}_{1} \left( 3 \right)\ \left\lbrack \alpha_{1} - \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}\ \left( \beta_{2} + \beta_{3} \right) \right\rbrack + \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}\ \beta_{3}\ {T''}_{1}\left( 4 \right)$$ [eq 3.3-130]

$$D_{4} = \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}\beta_{3}\ {T''}_{1}\left( 3 \right) + \ {T''}_{1} \left( 4 \right)\ \left\lbrack \alpha_{4} - \frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}\ \left( \beta_{3} + \beta_{4} \right) \right\rbrack + S_{\text{pr}}\ Q_{chch} \Delta t$$ [eq 3.3-131]

$${T''}_{1}\left( 1 \right) = T_{\text{e}1}\left( jp \right)$$ [eq 3.3-132]

$${T''}_{1}\left( 2 \right) = {\overline{T}}_{\text{c}1}\left( jc \right)$$ [eq 3.3-133]

$${T''}_{1}\left( 3 \right) = T_{\text{sti}1}\left( jc \right)$$ [eq 3.3-134]

and

$${T''}_{1}\left( 4 \right) = T_{\text{sto}1}\left( jc \right)$$ [eq 3.3-135]

This tri-diagonal matrix equation is solved in the same manner as in
Section 3.3.1.2 above. For step 3, Eq. (3.3-106) is rewritten as

$$T_{g2}\ = \frac{T_{g1} + d'\ s'}{1 + d'\ \theta_{2}}$$ [eq 3.3-136]

where

$$d'\ = \frac{2\pi\ r_{\text{brp}}\ H_{\text{eg}} \Delta t }{\left( \rho c \right)_{g}\ A_{g}}$$ [eq 3.3-137]

and

$$s' = \frac{\sum_{\text{jp}}{\left\{ \theta_{1}\ \left( T_{\text{c}1}\left( jp \right) - T_{\text{g}1} \right) + \theta_{2}\ T_{\text{e}2}\ \left( jp \right) \right\}\Delta z\left( jp \right)}}{\sum_{\text{jp}}{\Delta z\left( jp \right)}}$$ [eq 3.3-138]

[anchor: order-of-solution]

## 3.3.4. Order of Solution

For a time step, the coolant flow rates are calculated before any
temperatures are calculated. Extrapolated coolant temperatures are used
to obtain temperature-dependent coolant properties for the flow-rate
calculations. Section 3.9 describes the pre-voiding coolant flow-rate
calculations.

The order in which the temperature calculations are carried out depends
on whether the coolant flow is up or down. In either case, the
calculation goes in the direction of the flow. For upward flow, the
calculation starts at the subassembly inlet. The coolant temperature at
the first coolant node is set equal to the inlet temperatures,
 $T_{\text{in}}$ :

$$T_{\text{c}2}\left( 1 \right) = T_{\text{in}}$$ [eq 3.3-139]

The inlet temperature is supplied by the primary loop calculation, as
discussed in Chapter 5. For each axial coolant node, $jc$ , the coolant
temperature, $T_{\text{c}2} (jc)$ , at the bottom of the node is used as
input for the simultaneous calculation of temperatures at all radial
nodes. The solutions in Section 3.3.1, 3.3.2, and 3.3.3 provide
 ${\overline{T}}_{\text{c}2}\ \left( jc \right)$ , the
average coolant temperature for the axial node. The ${\overline{T}}_{\text{c}2} \left( jc + 1 \right)$ is obtained from

$$T_{c2} \left( jc + 1 \right) = 2{\overline{T}}_{\text{c}2} \left( jc \right) - T_{\text{c}2} \left( jc \right)$$ [eq 3.3-140]

This coolant temperature at the top of the node $jc$ is the coolant
inlet temperature used as input for the calculation at node $jc + 1$ .

If the flow is downward, the process is the same, except that the
calculation starts at the top of the subassembly and works down. The
coolant reentry temperatures, $T_{\text{up}\mathcal{l}}$ ,
described in Section 3.3.6 is used as the starting point for the last
coolant node MZC:

$$T_{\text{c}2} \left( \text{MZC} \right) = T_{\text{upl}}$$ [eq 3.3-141]

For each coolant node, $jc$ , $T_{\text{c}2} (jc + 1)$ is used as input
to the calculation of all radial nodes. The solutions in Section 3.3.1,
3.3.2, and 3.3.3 again provide
 ${\overline{T}}_{\text{c}2} \left( jc \right)$ .
Then $T_{\text{c}2} \left( jc \right)$ is obtained from

$$T_{\text{c}2} \left( jc \right) = 2{\overline{T}}_{\text{c}2} \left( jc \right) - T_{\text{c}2} \left( jc + 1 \right)$$ [eq 3.3-142]

[anchor: melting-of-fuel-or-cladding]

## 3.3.5. Melting of Fuel or Cladding

As discussed in Section 3.3.1.1, melting of both fuel and cladding is
treated with a melting range form a solidus temperature,
 $T_{\text{sol}}$ , to a liquidus temperature,
 $T_{\text{liq}}$ , rather than using a sharp melting temperature.
Between the solidus and the liquidus, an effective specific heat,
 $c_{\text{m}}$ , based on the heat of fusion is used, as in Eq. (3.3-3). In
the actual temperature calculations for a time step, the heat of fusion
is neglected. Then, at the end of the step the temperatures are modified
to account for the heat of fusion at any radial node that is going
through the melting range. A number of different cases are considered,
depending on the relationships between $T_1$ , the
temperature at the beginning of the step, and $T_2$ , the
temperature calculated for the end of the step, ignoring melting,
 $T_{\text{sol}}$ and $T_{\text{liq}}$ . In all cases,
 ${T''}_{2}$ is the final adjusted temperature at the end of the
step, and ${T'}_{2}$ would be the final adjusted temperature if it
did not go beyond the melting range.

Case 1: $T_{1} < T_{\mathrm{\text{sol}}}$ ,
 $T_{2} > T_{\mathrm{\text{sol}}}$ 

In this case, an adjusted temperature, ${T'}_{2}$ , is calculated
as

$${T'}_{2} = T_{\text{sol}} + \left( T_{2}\ - T_{\text{sol}} \right) \frac{c}{c_{\text{m}}}$$ [eq 3.3-143]

where c is the normal specific heat that was used in the calculation
of $T_2$ . Then,

$$\begin{split} {T}'_{2} = \begin{cases}
T_{\text{liq}} + \left( {T'}_{2}\ - T_{\text{liq}} \right) \frac{c_{\text{m}}}{c} & \text{if}\ \ {T'}_{2}\ \ > \ \ T_{\text{liq}} \\
{T'}_{2} & \text{otherwise} \\
\end{cases}\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-144]

In essence, Eq. (3.3-143) divides the energy above the solidus by
 $c_{\text{m}}$ to get the temperature above the solidus; and Eq. (3.3-144)
divides any energy above the liquidus by c to get the temperature
above the liquidus.

Case 2: $T_{1} > T_{\mathrm{\text{liq}}}$ ,
 $T_{2} < T_{\mathrm{\text{liq}}}$ 

In this case,

$${T'}_{2} = T_{\text{liq}} + \left( T_{2}- T_{\text{liq}} \right) \frac{c}{c_{\text{m}}}$$ [eq 3.3-145]

$$\begin{split} {T}'_{2} = \begin{cases}
T_{\text{sol}} + \left( {T'}_{2}\ - T_{\text{sol}} \right) \frac{c_{\text{m}}}{c} & \text{if}\ \ {T'}_{2}\ \ > \ \ T_{\text{sol}} \\
{T'}_{2} & \text{otherwise} \\
\end{cases}\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-146]

Case 3:
 $T_{\mathrm{\text{liq}}} \geq T_{1} \geq T_{\mathrm{\text{sol}}}$ 

In this case

$${T'}_{2} = T_{1} + \left( T_{2} - T_{1} \right) \frac{c}{c_{\text{m}}}$$ [eq 3.3-147]

$$\begin{split}{T'}_{2} = \begin{cases}
T_{\text{liq}} + \left( {T'}_{2} - T_{\text{liq}} \right) \frac{c_{m}}{c} & \text{if }{T'}_{2}\ \ > \ \ T_{\text{liq}} \\
T_{\text{sol}} + \left( {T'}_{2} - T_{\text{sol}} \right) \frac{c_{m}}{c} & \text{if }{T'}_{2}\ \ < \ \ T_{\text{sol}} \\
{T'}_{2} & \text{otherwise} \\
\end{cases}\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-148]

[anchor: coolant-inlet-and-re-entry-temperature]

## 3.3.6. Coolant Inlet and Re-entry Temperature

The coolant inlet temperature at the subassembly inlet and the re-entry
temperature at the subassembly outlet are determined mainly by the
coolant plenum temperatures calculated in PRIMAR-4; but, in addition,
mixing zones are modeled at the inlet and outlet of each channel.
Therefore, the temperature of coolant entering a subassembly is based on
the temperature in the part of the plenum in the immediate vicinity of
the end of the subassembly, and the reentry temperature soon after an
expulsion is based on both the bulk plenum temperature and the
temperature of the coolant that was recently expelled.

The mixing mode uses a mass, $M_{\text{mix}}$ , of the coolant in
the mixing volume, a specific heat, $C_{\text{mix}}$ , of the coolant, and
a time constant, $\tau_{\text{mix}}$ , for heat transfer or mixing
between the mixing volume and the bulk plenum coolant. If all
temperatures and flows are constant, then the mixing volume temperature,
 $T_{\text{mix}}$ , is assumed to approach an equilibrium value
asymptotically with an exponential decay. Figure 3.3.1 shows the model
for the outlet mixing volume.

If $T_{\text{mix}1}$ is the mixing volume temperature at the
beginning of a time step, the $T_{\text{mix}2}$ is the value at the
end, then

$$T_{\text{mix}2} = T_{\text{eq}} + \left( T_{\text{mix}1} - T_{\text{eq}} \right) e^{- \frac{\Delta t}{\tau}}$$ [eq 3.3-149]

For the outlet plenum,

$$\frac{1}{\tau} = \frac{1}{\tau_{\text{mix}}} + \frac{\left| \overline{w} \right|}{M_{\text{mix}}}$$ [eq 3.3-150]

where $\overline{w}$ is the average coolant flow rate
at the subassembly outlet. The equilibrium temperature is

Figure 3.3.1 Coolant Re-entry Temperature Model

$$\begin{split}T_{\text{eq}} = \begin{cases}
T_{\text{out}} & \text{if} \ \ \overline{w} \ \ \leq \ \ 0 \\
\left( \frac{T_{\text{out}}}{\tau_{\text{mix}}} + \frac{\overline{w} \overline{T}_{\text{exp}}}{M_{\text{mix}}} \right) \tau & \text{if} \ \ \overline{w} \ \ > \ \ 0 \\
\end{cases}\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-151]

where ${\overline{T}}_{\exp}$ is the average
temperature of the sodium expelled from the top of the subassembly into
the mixing volume during the time step. $T_{\text{out}}$ is the bulk
temperature in the outlet plenum. For the inlet plenum, the same
equations are used except that $\overline{w}$ changes
sign because positive flow results in flow from the inlet mixing volume
into the subassembly inlet. For the inlet, Eq. (3.3-151) and Eq. (3.3-151)
become

$$\begin{split}T_{\text{eq}} = \begin{cases}
T_{\text{in}} & \text{if} \ \ \overline{w} \ \ \leq \ \ 0 \\
\left( \frac{T_{\text{in}}}{\tau_{\text{mix}}} - \frac{\overline{w} \overline{T}_{\text{exp}}}{M_{\text{mix}}} \right) \tau & \text{if} \ \ \overline{w} \ \ > \ \ 0 \\
\end{cases}\end{split}$$ [eq 3.3-152]

where $T_{\text{in}}$ is the bulk temperature in the inlet
plenum.

The coolant inlet and reentry temperature calculations described above
are used in both the pre-voiding module and the boiling module except
when a vapor bubble has blown out of the top of the channel. In this
case, the condensation of vapor in the outlet plenum raises the
temperature in the mixing volume. A two-step process is used for each
time step. In the first step, vapor condensation raises the mixing
volume temperature. In the second step, heat transfer or mixing with the
bulk plenum coolant is accounted for. For the first step, a condensation
heat-transfer time constant, $\tau_{\text{c}}$ , is calculated as

$$\tau_{\text{c}} = \frac{M_{\text{mix}}\ C_{\text{mix}}}{h_{\text{cond}}\Delta Z_{\text{v}}\ p_{\text{er}}}$$ [eq 3.3-153]

where $h_{\text{cond}}$ is the vapor condensation heat-transfer
coefficient, $\Delta Z_{\text{v}}$ is the length of the vapor bubble beyond
the subassembly outlet, and $p_{\text{er}}$ is the bubble perimeter or
surface area per unit length. The value of $p_{\text{er}}$ is taken from
the coolant channel dimensions in the top node below the subassembly
outlet. For step 1, the new mixing volume temperature, $T_{\text{new}}$ ,
is calculated as

$$T_{\text{new}} = {\overline{T}}_{\text{v}} + \left( T_{mix1} - {\overline{T}}_{\text{v}} \right) e^{- \frac{\Delta t}{\tau_{\text{c}}}}$$ [eq 3.3-154]

where $\overline{T}_{v}$ is the average vapor
temperature in the part of the bubble above the subassembly outlet. In
the second step, the final mixing volume temperature is calculated as

$$T_{\text{mix}2} = T_{\text{new}} + \left( T_{\text{out}} - T_{\text{new}} \right) \left( 1 - e^{- \frac{\Delta t}{\tau_{\text{mix}}}} \right)$$ [eq 3.3-155]

[page: Part02/Ch03/steady-state-th.html]
